href="/wiki/Bishop" title="Bishop">bishop</a> surrounded by lions or in chains</td></tr><tr><th scope="row" class="infobox-label"><a href="/wiki/Patron_saint" title="Patron saint">Patronage</a></th><td class="infobox-data">Church in eastern Mediterranean; Church in North Africa</td></tr></tbody></table>
<p><b>Ignatius of Antioch</b> (<span class="rt-commentedText nowrap"><span class="IPA nopopups noexcerpt" lang="en-fonipa"><a href="/wiki/Help:IPA/English" title="Help:IPA/English">/<span style="border-bottom:1px dotted"><span title="/ɪ/: &#39;i&#39; in &#39;kit&#39;">ɪ</span><span title="/ɡ/: &#39;g&#39; in &#39;guy&#39;">ɡ</span><span title="/ˈ/: primary stress follows">ˈ</span><span title="&#39;n&#39; in &#39;nigh&#39;">n</span><span title="/eɪ/: &#39;a&#39; in &#39;face&#39;">eɪ</span><span title="/ʃ/: &#39;sh&#39; in &#39;shy&#39;">ʃ</span><span title="/ə/: &#39;a&#39; in &#39;about&#39;">ə</span><span title="&#39;s&#39; in &#39;sigh&#39;">s</span></span>/</a></span></span>; <a href="/wiki/Ancient_Greek" title="Ancient Greek">Greek</a>: Ἰγνάτιος Ἀντιοχείας, <i>Ignátios Antiokheías</i>; died c.&#160;108/140 AD),<sup id="cite_ref-pervo_3-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-pervo-3">&#91;3&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-date_of_ignatius_4-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-date_of_ignatius-4">&#91;4&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-8" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-8">&#91;8&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-9" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-9">&#91;9&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-10" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-10">&#91;10&#93;</a></sup> also known as <b>Ignatius Theophorus</b> (<span title="Ancient Greek (to 1453)-language text"><span lang="grc">Ἰγνάτιος ὁ Θεοφόρος</span></span>, <i>Ignátios ho Theophóros</i>, <small>lit.</small> "the God-bearing"), was an early Christian writer and <a href="/wiki/Patriarch_of_Antioch" title="Patriarch of Antioch">Patriarch of Antioch</a>. While en route to <a href="/wiki/Rome" title="Rome">Rome</a>, where he met his <a href="/wiki/Christian_martyrs" class="mw-redirect" title="Christian martyrs">martyrdom</a>, Ignatius wrote a series of letters. This correspondence now forms a central part of a later collection of works known to be authored by the <a href="/wiki/Apostolic_Fathers" title="Apostolic Fathers">Apostolic Fathers</a>. He is considered to be one of the three most important of these, together with <a href="/wiki/Clement_of_Rome" title="Clement of Rome">Clement of Rome</a> and <a href="/wiki/Polycarp" title="Polycarp">Polycarp</a>. His letters also serve as an example of early <a href="/wiki/Christian_theology" title="Christian theology">Christian theology</a>. Important topics they address include <a href="/wiki/Ecclesiology" title="Ecclesiology">ecclesiology</a>, the <a href="/wiki/Sacraments" class="mw-redirect" title="Sacraments">sacraments</a>, and the role of <a href="/wiki/Bishops" class="mw-redirect" title="Bishops">bishops</a>. 
</p>
<meta property="mw:PageProp/toc" />
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Life">Life</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Ignatius_of_Antioch&amp;action=edit&amp;section=1" title="Edit section: Life">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r1129693374">.mw-parser-output .hlist dl,.mw-parser-output .hlist ol,.mw-parser-output .hlist ul{margin:0;padding:0}.mw-parser-output .hlist Related epistles</th></tr><tr><td class="sidebar-content">
<a href="/wiki/Epistle_of_Polycarp_to_the_Philippians" title="Epistle of Polycarp to the Philippians">Epistle of Polycarp to the Philippians</a></td>
</tr><tr><td class="sidebar-navbar"><link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1129693374"/><style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r1063604349">.mw-parser-output .navbar{display:inline;font-size:88%;font-weight:normal}.mw-parser-output .navbar-collapse{float:left;text-align:left}.mw-parser-output .navbar-boxtext{word-spacing:0}.mw-parser-output .navbar ul{display:inline-block;white-space:nowrap;line-height:inherit}.mw-parser-output .navbar-brackets::before{margin-right:-0.125em;content:"[ "}.mw-parser-output .navbar-brackets::after{margin-left:-0.125em;content:" ]"}.mw-parser-output .navbar li{word-spacing:-0.125em}.mw-parser-output .navbar a>span,.mw-parser-output .navbar a>abbr{text-decoration:inherit}.mw-parser-output .navbar-mini abbr{font-variant:small-caps;border-bottom:none;text-decoration:none;cursor:inherit}.mw-parser-output .navbar-ct-full{font-size:114%;margin:0 7em}.mw-parser-output .navbar-ct-mini{font-size:114%;margin:0 4em}</style><div class="navbar plainlinks hlist navbar-mini"><ul><li class="nv-view"><a href="/wiki/Template:Ignatius_of_Antioch" title="Template:Ignatius of Antioch"><abbr title="View this template">v</abbr></a></li><li class="nv-talk"><a href="/wiki/Template_talk:Ignatius_of_Antioch" title="Template talk:Ignatius of Antioch"><abbr title="Discuss this template">t</abbr></a></li><li class="nv-edit"><a class="external text" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Template:Ignatius_of_Antioch&amp;action=edit"><abbr title="Edit this template">e</abbr></a></li></ul></div></td></tr></tbody></table>
<p>Nothing is known of Ignatius' life apart from what may be inferred internally from his letters, except from later (sometimes spurious) traditions. It is said Ignatius <a href="/wiki/Conversion_(religious)" class="mw-redirect" title="Conversion (religious)">converted</a> to Christianity<sup id="cite_ref-11" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-11">&#91;11&#93;</a></sup> at a young age. Tradition identifies Ignatius, along with his friend <a href="/wiki/Polycarp" title="Polycarp">Polycarp</a>, as disciples of <a href="/wiki/John_the_Apostle" title="John the Apostle">John the Apostle</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-Catholic_Encyclopedia_12-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Catholic_Encyclopedia-12">&#91;12&#93;</a></sup> Later in his life, Ignatius was chosen to serve as <a href="/wiki/Bishop_of_Antioch" class="mw-redirect" title="Bishop of Antioch">Bishop of Antioch</a>; the fourth-century Church historian <a href="/wiki/Eusebius" title="Eusebius">Eusebius</a> writes that Ignatius succeeded <a href="/wiki/Evodius" title="Evodius">Evodius</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-13" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-13">&#91;13&#93;</a></sup> <a href="/wiki/Theodoret" title="Theodoret">Theodoret</a> of <a href="/wiki/Cyrrhus" title="Cyrrhus">Cyrrhus</a> claimed that St. Peter himself left directions that Ignatius be appointed to the <a href="/wiki/Episcopal_see" title="Episcopal see">episcopal see</a> of <a href="/wiki/Antioch" title="Antioch">Antioch</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-crawley_14-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-crawley-14">&#91;14&#93;</a></sup> Ignatius called himself <i>Theophorus</i> (God Bearer). A tradition arose that he was one of the children whom Jesus Christ took in his arms and blessed.<sup id="cite_ref-The_Martyrdom_of_Ignatius_15-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-The_Martyrdom_of_Ignatius-15">&#91;15&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Veneration">Veneration</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Ignatius_of_Antioch&amp;action=edit&amp;section=2" title="Edit section: Veneration">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<p>Ignatius' <a href="/wiki/Calendar_of_saints" title="Calendar of saints">feast day</a> was kept in his own Antioch on 17 October, the day on which he is now celebrated in the Catholic Church and generally in <a href="/wiki/Western_Christianity" title="Western Christianity">western Christianity</a>, although from the 12th century until 1969 it was put at 1 February in the <a href="/wiki/General_Roman_Calendar" title="General Roman Calendar">General Roman Calendar</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-16" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-16">&#91;16&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-17" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-17">&#91;17&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In the <a href="/wiki/Eastern_Orthodox_Church" title="Eastern Orthodox Church">Eastern Orthodox Church</a> it is observed on 20 December.<sup id="cite_ref-18" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-18">&#91;18&#93;</a></sup> The <a href="/wiki/Synaxarium" title="Synaxarium">Synaxarium</a> of the <a href="/wiki/Coptic_Orthodox_Church_of_Alexandria" class="mw-redirect" title="Coptic Orthodox Church of Alexandria">Coptic Orthodox Church of Alexandria</a> places it on the 24th of the Coptic Month of <a href="/wiki/Koiak" title="Koiak">Koiak</a> (which is also the 24th day of the fourth month of Tahisas in the <a href="/wiki/Synaxarium" title="Synaxarium">Synaxarium</a> of The <a href="/wiki/Ethiopian_Orthodox_Tewahedo_Church" title="Ethiopian Orthodox Tewahedo Church">Ethiopian Orthodox Tewahedo Church</a>), corresponding in three years out of every four to 20 December in the <a href="/wiki/Julian_Calendar" class="mw-redirect" title="Julian Calendar">Julian Calendar</a>, which currently falls on 2 January of the <a href="/wiki/Gregorian_Calendar" class="mw-redirect" title="Gregorian Calendar">Gregorian Calendar</a>.
</p><p>Ignatius is honored in the <a href="/wiki/Calendar_of_saints_(Church_of_England)" title="Calendar of saints (Church of England)">Church of England</a> and in the <a href="/wiki/Calendar_of_saints_(Episcopal_Church)" title="Calendar of saints (Episcopal Church)">Episcopal Church</a> on <a href="/wiki/October_17" title="October 17">17 October</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-19" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-19">&#91;19&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-20" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-20">&#91;20&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Martyrdom">Martyrdom</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Ignatius_of_Antioch&amp;action=edit&amp;section=3" title="Edit section: Martyrdom">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Circumstances_of_martyrdom">Circumstances of martyrdom</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Ignatius_of_Antioch&amp;action=edit&amp;section=4" title="Edit section: Circumstances of martyrdom">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>Instead of being executed in his home town of Antioch, Ignatius was escorted to Rome by a company of ten Roman soldiers:
</p>
<style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r996844942">.mw-parser-output .templatequote{overflow:hidden;margin:1em 0;padding:0 40px}.mw-parser-output .templatequote .templatequotecite{line-height:1.5em;text-align:left;padding-left:1.6em;margin-top:0}</style><blockquote class="templatequote"><p>From Syria even unto Rome I fight with beasts, both by land and sea, both by night and day, being bound to ten leopards, I mean a band of soldiers...</p><div class="templatequotecite">—&#8201;<cite><i><a rel="nofollow" class="external text" href="http://www.newadvent.org/fathers/0107.htm">Ignatius to the Romans</a></i> Chapter 5</cite></div></blockquote>
<p>Scholars consider Ignatius' transport to Rome unusual, since those <a href="/wiki/Persecution_of_Christians_in_the_Roman_Empire" title="Persecution of Christians in the Roman Empire">persecuted as Christians</a> would be expected to be punished locally. Stevan Davies has pointed out that "no other examples exist from the <a href="/wiki/Flavian_dynasty" title="Flavian dynasty">Flavian age</a> of any prisoners except citizens or prisoners of war being brought to Rome for execution."<sup id="cite_ref-davies_21-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-davies-21">&#91;21&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>If Ignatius had been a Roman citizen, he could have appealed to the emperor, but then he would usually have been beheaded rather than tortured.<sup id="cite_ref-Arnold_2017_p._38_22-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Arnold_2017_p._38-22">&#91;22&#93;</a></sup> Furthermore, the epistles of Ignatius state that he was put in chains during the journey to Rome, but it was illegal under Roman law for a citizen to be put in bonds during an appeal to the emperor.<sup id="cite_ref-davies_21-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-davies-21">&#91;21&#93;</a></sup><sup class="reference nowrap"><span title="Page / location: 175–176">&#58;&#8202;175–176&#8202;</span></sup>
</p><p><a href="/wiki/Allen_Brent" title="Allen Brent">Allen Brent</a> argues that Ignatius was transferred to Rome at the request of the emperor in order to provide entertainment to the masses by being killed in the <a href="/wiki/Colosseum" title="Colosseum">Colosseum</a>. Brent insists, contrary to some, that "it was normal practice to transport condemned criminals from the provinces in order to offer spectator sport in the Colosseum at Rome."<sup id="cite_ref-brent_23-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-brent-23">&#91;23&#93;</a></sup><sup class="reference nowrap"><span title="Page / location: 15">&#58;&#8202;15&#8202;</span></sup>
</p><p>Stevan Davies rejects the idea that Ignatius was transported to Rome for the games at the Colosseum. He reasons that "if Ignatius was in some way a donation by the Imperial Governor of Syria to the games at Rome, a single prisoner seems a rather miserly gift."<sup id="cite_ref-davies_21-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-davies-21">&#91;21&#93;</a></sup><sup class="reference nowrap"><span title="Page / location: 176">&#58;&#8202;176&#8202;</span></sup> Instead, Davies proposes that Ignatius may have been indicted by a legate, or representative, of the <a href="/wiki/Roman_governor" title="Roman governor">governor</a> of <a href="/wiki/Roman_Syria" title="Roman Syria">Syria</a> while the governor was away temporarily, and sent to Rome for trial and execution. Under Roman law, only the governor of a province or the emperor himself could impose <a href="/wiki/Capital_punishment" title="Capital punishment">capital punishment</a>, so the legate would have faced the choice of imprisoning Ignatius in Antioch or sending him to Rome. Davies postulates that the legate may have decided to send Ignatius to Rome so as to minimize any further dissension among the Antiochene Christians.<sup id="cite_ref-davies_21-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-davies-21">&#91;21&#93;</a></sup><sup class="reference nowrap"><span title="Page / location: 177–178">&#58;&#8202;177–178&#8202;</span></sup>
</p><p>Christine Trevett has called Davies' suggestion "entirely hypothetical" and concludes that no fully satisfactory solution to the problem can be found, writing, "I tend to take the bishop at his word when he says he is a condemned man. But the question remains, why is he going to Rome? The truth is that we do not know."<sup id="cite_ref-trevett_24-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-trevett-24">&#91;24&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Route_of_travel_to_Rome">Route of travel to Rome</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Ignatius_of_Antioch&amp;action=edit&amp;section=5" title="Edit section: Route of travel to Rome">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>During the journey to Rome, Ignatius and his entourage of soldiers made a number of lengthy stops in <a href="/wiki/Asia_Minor" class="mw-redirect" title="Asia Minor">Asia Minor</a>, deviating from the most direct land route from Antioch to Rome.<sup id="cite_ref-davies_21-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-davies-21">&#91;21&#93;</a></sup><sup class="reference nowrap"><span title="Page / location: 176">&#58;&#8202;176&#8202;</span></sup> Scholars generally agree on the following reconstruction of Ignatius' route of travel:
</p>
<ol><li>Ignatius first traveled from Antioch, in the province of <a href="/wiki/Roman_Syria" title="Roman Syria">Syria</a>, to Asia Minor. It is uncertain whether he traveled by sea or by land.</li>
<li>He was then taken to <a href="/wiki/Smyrna" title="Smyrna">Smyrna</a>, via a route that bypassed the cities of <a href="/wiki/Magnesia_on_the_Meander" class="mw-redirect" title="Magnesia on the Meander">Magnesia</a>, <a href="/wiki/Tralles" class="mw-redirect" title="Tralles">Tralles</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Ephesus" title="Ephesus">Ephesus</a>, but likely passed through <a href="/wiki/Ala%C5%9Fehir" title="Alaşehir">Philadelphia</a> (cf. <a rel="nofollow" class="external text" href="http://www.newadvent.org/fathers/0108.htm">Ign. Phil.</a> 7).</li>
<li>Ignatius then traveled to <a href="/wiki/Alexandria_Troas" title="Alexandria Troas">Troas</a>, where he boarded a ship bound for <a href="/wiki/Neapolis_(Thrace)" title="Neapolis (Thrace)">Neapolis</a> in <a href="/wiki/Macedonia_(Greece)" title="Macedonia (Greece)">Macedonia</a> (cf. <a rel="nofollow" class="external text" href="http://www.newadvent.org/fathers/0110.htm">Ign. Pol.</a> 8).</li>
<li>He then passed through the city of <a href="/wiki/Philippi" title="Philippi">Philippi</a> (cf. <a rel="nofollow" class="external text" href="http://www.newadvent.org/fathers/0136.htm">Pol. Phil.</a> 9).</li>
<li>After this, he took some land or sea route to Rome.<sup id="cite_ref-jefford_25-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-jefford-25">&#91;25&#93;</a></sup></li></ol>
<p>During the journey, the soldiers seem to have allowed Ignatius to meet with entire congregations of Christians while in chains, at least while he was in <a href="/wiki/Ala%C5%9Fehir" title="Alaşehir">Philadelphia</a> (cf. <a rel="nofollow" class="external text" href="http://www.newadvent.org/fathers/0108.htm">Ign. Phil.</a> 7), and numerous Christian visitors and messengers were allowed to meet with him on a one-on-one basis. These messengers allowed Ignatius to send six letters to nearby churches, and one to <a href="/wiki/Polycarp" title="Polycarp">Polycarp</a>, the bishop of <a href="/wiki/Smyrna" title="Smyrna">Smyrna</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-davies_21-5" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-davies-21">&#91;21&#93;</a></sup><sup class="reference nowrap"><span title="Page / location: 176">&#58;&#8202;176&#8202;</span></sup>
</p><p>These aspects of Ignatius' martyrdom are also regarded by scholars as unusual. It is generally expected that a prisoner would be transported on the most direct, cost-effective route to their destination. Since travel by land in the <a href="/wiki/Roman_Empire" title="Roman Empire">Roman Empire</a> was between five and fifty-two times more expensive than travel by sea,<sup id="cite_ref-oxford_26-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-oxford-26">&#91;26&#93;</a></sup> and Antioch was a major port city, the most efficient route would likely have been entirely by sea. Steven Davies argues that Ignatius' circuitous route to Rome can only be explained by positing that he was not the main purpose of the soldiers' trip, and that the various stops in Asia Minor were for other state business. He suggests that such a scenario would also explain the relative freedom that Ignatius was given to meet with other Christians during the journey.<sup id="cite_ref-davies_21-6" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-davies-21">&#91;21&#93;</a></sup><sup class="reference nowrap"><span title="Page / location: 177">&#58;&#8202;177&#8202;</span></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Date_of_martyrdom">Date of martyrdom</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Ignatius_of_Antioch&amp;action=edit&amp;section=6" title="Edit section: Date of martyrdom">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>Due to the sparse and fragmentary nature of the documentation of Ignatius' life and martyrdom, the date of his death is subject to a significant amount of uncertainty. Tradition places the martyrdom of Ignatius in the reign of <a href="/wiki/Trajan" title="Trajan">Trajan</a>, who was emperor of Rome from 98 to 117 AD. The earliest source for this Trajanic date is the 4th century church historian <a href="/wiki/Eusebius_of_Caesarea" class="mw-redirect" title="Eusebius of Caesarea">Eusebius of Caesarea</a>, who is regarded by some modern scholars as an unreliable source for chronological information regarding the early church. It has been argued that Eusebius had an ideological interest in dating church leaders as early as possible, and ensuring that there were no gaps in succession between the original apostles of Jesus and the leaders of the church in his day.<sup id="cite_ref-pervo_3-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-pervo-3">&#91;3&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>While many scholars accept the traditional dating of Ignatius' martyrdom under Trajan, others have argued for a somewhat later date. <a href="/wiki/Richard_Pervo" title="Richard Pervo">Richard Pervo</a> dated Ignatius' death to 135–140 AD.<sup id="cite_ref-pervo_3-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-pervo-3">&#91;3&#93;</a></sup> British <a href="/wiki/Classics" title="Classics">classicist</a> <a href="/wiki/Timothy_Barnes_(classicist)" title="Timothy Barnes (classicist)">Timothy Barnes</a> has argued for a date in the 140s AD, on the grounds that Ignatius seems to have quoted a work of the <a href="/wiki/Gnosticism" title="Gnosticism">Gnostic</a> <a href="/wiki/Ptolemy_(gnostic)" class="mw-redirect" title="Ptolemy (gnostic)">Ptolemy</a> in one of his epistles, who only became active in the 130s.<sup id="cite_ref-date_of_ignatius_4-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-date_of_ignatius-4">&#91;4&#93;</a></sup> Étienne Decrept has argued on the basis of the witnesses of <a href="/wiki/John_Malalas" title="John Malalas">John Malalas</a> and the <i>Acts of Drosis</i> that Ignatius, along with other Christians, was martyred under the reign of Trajan, but during Apollo's festival in July 116 AD and in response to the earthquake that had struck Antioch at the end of 115 AD.<sup id="cite_ref-27" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-27">&#91;27&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Death_and_aftermath">Death and aftermath</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Ignatius_of_Antioch&amp;action=edit&amp;section=7" title="Edit section: Death and aftermath">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>Ignatius himself wrote that he would be thrown to the beasts, and in the fourth century Eusebius reports tradition that this came to pass,<sup id="cite_ref-Eusebius_3.36_28-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Eusebius_3.36-28">&#91;28&#93;</a></sup> which is then repeated by <a href="/wiki/Jerome" title="Jerome">Jerome</a> who is the first to explicitly mention "lions."<sup id="cite_ref-Arnold_2017_p._38_22-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Arnold_2017_p._38-22">&#91;22&#93;</a></sup> <a href="/wiki/John_Chrysostom" title="John Chrysostom">John Chrysostom</a> is the first to allude to the <a href="/wiki/Colosseum" title="Colosseum">Colosseum</a> as the place of Ignatius' martyrdom.<sup id="cite_ref-29" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-29">&#91;29&#93;</a></sup> Contemporary scholars are uncertain that any of these authors had sources other than Ignatius' own writings.<sup id="cite_ref-Arnold_2017_p._38_22-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Arnold_2017_p._38-22">&#91;22&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-Eusebius_3.36_28-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Eusebius_3.36-28">&#91;28&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>According to a medieval Christian text titled <i>Martyrium Ignatii</i>, Ignatius' remains were carried back to Antioch by his companions after his martyrdom.<sup id="cite_ref-ante_30-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ante-30">&#91;30&#93;</a></sup> The sixth-century writings of <a href="/wiki/Evagrius_Scholasticus" title="Evagrius Scholasticus">Evagrius Scholasticus</a> state that the reputed remains of Ignatius were moved by the Emperor <a href="/wiki/Theodosius_II" title="Theodosius II">Theodosius II</a> to the Tychaeum, or Temple of <a href="/wiki/Tyche" title="Tyche">Tyche</a>, which had been converted into a church dedicated to Ignatius.<sup id="cite_ref-31" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-31">&#91;31&#93;</a></sup> In 637, when <a href="/wiki/Battle_of_the_Iron_Bridge" title="Battle of the Iron Bridge">Antioch was captured</a> by the <a href="/wiki/Rashidun_Caliphate" title="Rashidun Caliphate">Rashidun Caliphate</a>, the <a href="/wiki/Relic" title="Relic">relics</a> were transferred to the <a href="/wiki/Basilica_di_San_Clemente" class="mw-redirect" title="Basilica di San Clemente">Basilica di San Clemente</a> in Rome.<sup id="cite_ref-32" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-32">&#91;32&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="The_Martyrium_Ignatii">The <i>Martyrium Ignatii</i></span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Ignatius_of_Antioch&amp;action=edit&amp;section=8" title="Edit section: The Martyrium Ignatii">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>There is a purported eye-witness account of his martyrdom, named the <i><a href="/wiki/Martyrium_Ignatii" title="Martyrium Ignatii">Martyrium Ignatii</a></i>.<sup id="cite_ref-ante_30-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ante-30">&#91;30&#93;</a></sup> It is presented as being an eye-witness account for the church of Antioch, attributed to Ignatius' companions, <a href="/wiki/Philo_of_Cilicia" title="Philo of Cilicia">Philo of Cilicia</a>, deacon at <a href="/wiki/Tarsus,_Mersin" title="Tarsus, Mersin">Tarsus</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Rheus_Agathopus" title="Rheus Agathopus">Rheus Agathopus</a>, a Syrian.<sup id="cite_ref-jefford_25-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-jefford-25">&#91;25&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Its most reliable manuscript is the 10th-century <i><a href="/wiki/Codex_Colbertinus" title="Codex Colbertinus">Codex Colbertinus</a></i> (Paris), in which the <i>Martyrium</i> closes the collection. The <i>Martyrium</i> presents the confrontation of the bishop Ignatius with <a href="/wiki/Trajan" title="Trajan">Trajan</a> at Antioch, a familiar <a href="/wiki/Trope_(literature)" title="Trope (literature)">trope</a> of <i>Acta</i> of the martyrs, and many details of the long, partly overland voyage to Rome. The <a href="/wiki/Synaxarium" title="Synaxarium">Synaxarium</a> of the <a href="/wiki/Coptic_Orthodox_Church_of_Alexandria" class="mw-redirect" title="Coptic Orthodox Church of Alexandria">Coptic Orthodox Church of Alexandria</a> says that he was thrown to the wild beasts that devoured him and rent him to pieces.<sup id="cite_ref-synaxarium_33-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-synaxarium-33">&#91;33&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Epistles">Epistles</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Ignatius_of_Antioch&amp;action=edit&amp;section=9" title="Edit section: Epistles">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:302px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Ignatius_of_Antioch.jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/ba/Ignatius_of_Antioch.jpg/300px-Ignatius_of_Antioch.jpg" decoding="async" width="300" height="206" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/ba/Ignatius_of_Antioch.jpg/450px-Ignatius_of_Antioch.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/ba/Ignatius_of_Antioch.jpg/600px-Ignatius_of_Antioch.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1949" data-file-height="1341" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Ignatius_of_Antioch.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>An icon of Ignatius of Antioch from the <a href="/wiki/Menologion_of_Basil_II" title="Menologion of Basil II">Menologion of Basil II</a> (c. 1000 AD)</div></div></div>
<p>The following seven epistles preserved under the name of Ignatius are generally considered authentic, since they were mentioned by the historian <a href="/wiki/Eusebius" title="Eusebius">Eusebius</a> in the first half of the fourth century.
</p><p><b>Seven original epistles:</b>
</p>
<ul><li>The <a href="/wiki/Epistle_of_Ignatius_to_the_Ephesians" title="Epistle of Ignatius to the Ephesians">Epistle to the Ephesians</a>,</li>
<li>The <a href="/wiki/Epistle_of_Ignatius_to_the_Magnesians" title="Epistle of Ignatius to the Magnesians">Epistle to the Magnesians</a>,</li>
<li>The <a href="/wiki/Epistle_of_Ignatius_to_the_Trallians" title="Epistle of Ignatius to the Trallians">Epistle to the Trallians</a>,</li>
<li>The <a href="/wiki/Epistle_of_Ignatius_to_the_Romans" title="Epistle of Ignatius to the Romans">Epistle to the Romans</a>,</li>
<li>The <a href="/wiki/Epistle_of_Ignatius_to_the_Philadelphians" title="Epistle of Ignatius to the Philadelphians">Epistle to the Philadelphians</a>,</li>
<li>The <a href="/wiki/Epistle_of_Ignatius_to_the_Smyrnaeans" title="Epistle of Ignatius to the Smyrnaeans">Epistle to the Smyrnaeans</a>,</li>
<li>The <a href="/wiki/Epistle_of_Ignatius_to_Polycarp" title="Epistle of Ignatius to Polycarp">Epistle to Polycarp</a>, a <a href="/wiki/Bishop" title="Bishop">bishop</a> of <a href="/wiki/Smyrna" title="Smyrna">Smyrna</a>.</li></ul>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Recensions">Recensions</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Ignatius_of_Antioch&amp;action=edit&amp;section=10" title="Edit section: Recensions">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>The text of these epistles is known in three different <a href="/wiki/Recension" title="Recension">recensions</a>, or editions: the Short Recension, found in a <a href="/wiki/Syriac_language" title="Syriac language">Syriac</a> <a href="/wiki/Manuscript" title="Manuscript">manuscript</a>; the Middle Recension, found only in <a href="/wiki/Ancient_Greek" title="Ancient Greek">Greek</a> manuscripts; and the Long Recension, found in Greek and <a href="/wiki/Latin" title="Latin">Latin</a> manuscripts.<sup id="cite_ref-date_of_ignatius_4-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-date_of_ignatius-4">&#91;4&#93;</a></sup><sup class="reference nowrap"><span title="Page / location: 120–121">&#58;&#8202;120–121&#8202;</span></sup><sup id="cite_ref-Quasten1-80-72_34-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Quasten1-80-72-34">&#91;34&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>For some time, it was believed that the Long Recension was the only extant version of the Ignatian epistles, but around 1628 a Latin translation of the Middle Recension was discovered by <a href="/wiki/Archbishop" title="Archbishop">Archbishop</a> <a href="/wiki/James_Ussher" title="James Ussher">James Ussher</a>, who published it in 1646. For around a quarter of a century after this, it was debated which recension represented the original text of the epistles. But ever since <a href="/wiki/John_Pearson_(bishop)" title="John Pearson (bishop)">John Pearson</a>'s strong defense of the authenticity of the Middle Recension in the late 17th century, there has been a scholarly consensus that the Middle Recension is the original version of the text.<sup id="cite_ref-date_of_ignatius_4-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-date_of_ignatius-4">&#91;4&#93;</a></sup><sup class="reference nowrap"><span title="Page / location: 121">&#58;&#8202;121&#8202;</span></sup> The Long Recension is the product of a fourth-century <a href="/wiki/Arianism" title="Arianism">Arian</a> Christian, who <a href="/wiki/Interpolation_(manuscripts)" title="Interpolation (manuscripts)">interpolated</a> the Middle Recension epistles in order posthumously to enlist Ignatius as an unwitting witness in theological disputes of that age. This individual also forged the six spurious epistles attributed to Ignatius (see <a href="#Pseudo-Ignatius">§&#160;Pseudo-Ignatius</a> below).<sup id="cite_ref-trobisch-2007_35-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-trobisch-2007-35">&#91;35&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Manuscripts representing the Short Recension of the Ignatian epistles were discovered and published by <a href="/wiki/William_Cureton" title="William Cureton">William Cureton</a> in the mid-19th century. For a brief period, there was a scholarly debate on the question of whether the Short Recension was earlier and more original than the Middle Recension. But by the end of the 19th century, <a href="/wiki/Theodor_Zahn" title="Theodor Zahn">Theodor Zahn</a> and <a href="/wiki/J._B._Lightfoot" title="J. B. Lightfoot">J. B. Lightfoot</a> had established a scholarly consensus that the Short Recension is merely a summary of the text of the Middle Recension, and was therefore composed later.<sup id="cite_ref-date_of_ignatius_4-5" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-date_of_ignatius-4">&#91;4&#93;</a></sup><sup class="reference nowrap"><span title="Page / location: 121">&#58;&#8202;121&#8202;</span></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Authenticity">Authenticity</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Ignatius_of_Antioch&amp;action=edit&amp;section=11" title="Edit section: Authenticity">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>Ever since the <a href="/wiki/Reformation" title="Reformation">Protestant Reformation</a> in the 16th century, the authenticity of all the Ignatian epistles has come under intense scrutiny. <a href="/wiki/John_Calvin" title="John Calvin">John Calvin</a> called the epistles "rubbish published under Ignatius’ name."<sup id="cite_ref-date_of_ignatius_4-6" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-date_of_ignatius-4">&#91;4&#93;</a></sup><sup class="reference nowrap"><span title="Page / location: 119">&#58;&#8202;119&#8202;</span></sup> Some Protestants have tended to want to deny the authenticity of all the epistles attributed to Ignatius because they seem to attest to the existence of a <a href="/wiki/Monarchical_episcopate" class="mw-redirect" title="Monarchical episcopate">monarchical episcopate</a> in the second century. The Catholic Church has long held up the authenticity of the letters from past to present.<sup id="cite_ref-36" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-36">&#91;36&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In 1886, <a href="/wiki/Presbyterian" class="mw-redirect" title="Presbyterian">Presbyterian</a> minister and church historian <a href="/wiki/William_Dool_Killen" title="William Dool Killen">William Dool Killen</a> published an essay extensively arguing that none of the epistles attributed to Ignatius were authentic. Instead, he argued that <a href="/wiki/Pope_Callixtus_I" title="Pope Callixtus I">Callixtus</a>, bishop of Rome, <a href="/wiki/Pseudepigrapha" title="Pseudepigrapha">forged</a> the letters around AD 220 to garner support for a <a href="/wiki/Apostolic_succession" title="Apostolic succession">monarchical episcopate</a>, modeling the renowned Saint Ignatius after his own life to give precedent for his own authority.<sup id="cite_ref-Killen_1886_37-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Killen_1886-37">&#91;37&#93;</a></sup><sup class="reference nowrap"><span title="Page / location: 137">&#58;&#8202;137&#8202;</span></sup> Killen contrasted this <a href="/wiki/Episcopal_polity" title="Episcopal polity">episcopal polity</a> with the <a href="/wiki/Presbyterian_polity" title="Presbyterian polity">presbyterian polity</a> in the writings of <a href="/wiki/Polycarp" title="Polycarp">Polycarp</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-Killen_1886_37-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Killen_1886-37">&#91;37&#93;</a></sup><sup class="reference nowrap"><span title="Page / location: 127">&#58;&#8202;127&#8202;</span></sup>
</p><p>Some doubts about the authenticity of the original letters continued into the 20th century. In the late 1970s and 1980s, the scholars <a href="/wiki/Robert_Joly" title="Robert Joly">Robert Joly</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-38" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-38">&#91;38&#93;</a></sup> Reinhard Hübner,<sup id="cite_ref-39" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-39">&#91;39&#93;</a></sup> <a href="/wiki/Markus_Vinzent" title="Markus Vinzent">Markus Vinzent</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-40" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-40">&#91;40&#93;</a></sup> and Thomas Lechner<sup id="cite_ref-41" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-41">&#91;41&#93;</a></sup> argued forcefully that the epistles of the Middle Recension were forgeries written during the reign of <a href="/wiki/Marcus_Aurelius" title="Marcus Aurelius">Marcus Aurelius</a> (161–180 AD). Around the same time, the scholar Joseph Ruis-Camps published a study arguing that the Middle Recension letters were <a href="/wiki/Pseudepigrapha" title="Pseudepigrapha">pseudepigraphically</a> composed based on an original, smaller, authentic corpus of four letters (<a href="/wiki/Epistle_of_Ignatius_to_the_Romans" title="Epistle of Ignatius to the Romans">Romans</a>, <a href="/wiki/Epistle_of_Ignatius_to_the_Magnesians" title="Epistle of Ignatius to the Magnesians">Magnesians</a>, <a href="/wiki/Epistle_of_Ignatius_to_the_Trallians" title="Epistle of Ignatius to the Trallians">Trallians</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Epistle_of_Ignatius_to_the_Ephesians" title="Epistle of Ignatius to the Ephesians">Ephesians</a>). In 2009, Otto Zwierlein took up the thesis of a fake, made around 170.<sup id="cite_ref-42" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-42">&#91;42&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>These publications stirred up tremendous, heated controversy in the scholarly community at the time,<sup id="cite_ref-date_of_ignatius_4-7" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-date_of_ignatius-4">&#91;4&#93;</a></sup><sup class="reference nowrap"><span title="Page / location: 122">&#58;&#8202;122&#8202;</span></sup> but in 2017, most patristic scholars accepted the authenticity of the seven original epistles.<sup id="cite_ref-date_of_ignatius_4-8" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-date_of_ignatius-4">&#91;4&#93;</a></sup><sup class="reference nowrap"><span title="Page / location: 121ff">&#58;&#8202;121ff&#8202;</span></sup><sup id="cite_ref-43" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-43">&#91;43&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-44" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-44">&#91;44&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-45" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-45">&#91;45&#93;</a></sup> However, J. Lookadoo said in 2020 that "the debate has received renewed energy since the late 1990s and shows few signs of slowing."<sup id="cite_ref-46" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-46">&#91;46&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The original texts of six of the seven original letters are found in the <a href="/wiki/Codex_Mediceo_Laurentianus" class="mw-redirect" title="Codex Mediceo Laurentianus">Codex Mediceo Laurentianus</a> written in Greek in the 11th century (which also contains the pseudepigraphical letters of the Long Recension, except that to the Philippians),<sup id="cite_ref-47" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-47">&#91;47&#93;</a></sup> while the letter to the Romans is found in the <a href="/wiki/Codex_Colbertinus" title="Codex Colbertinus">Codex Colbertinus</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-Catholic_Encyclopedia_12-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Catholic_Encyclopedia-12">&#91;12&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Style_and_structure">Style and structure</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Ignatius_of_Antioch&amp;action=edit&amp;section=12" title="Edit section: Style and structure">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>Ignatius's letters bear signs of being written in great haste and without a proper plan, such as <a href="/wiki/Run-on_sentences" class="mw-redirect" title="Run-on sentences">run-on sentences</a> and an unsystematic succession of thought. Ignatius modelled his writings after those written by Paul, Peter, and John, and even quoted or paraphrased biblical entries by these apostles' works freely, such as when he quoted 1 Corinthians 1:18, in his letter to the Ephesians:
</p>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r996844942"/><blockquote class="templatequote"><p>Let my spirit be counted as nothing for the sake of the cross, which is a stumbling-block to those that do not believe, but to us salvation and life eternal.</p><div class="templatequotecite">—&#8201;<cite><i>Letter to the Ephesians</i> 18, Roberts and Donaldson translation<sup id="cite_ref-48" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-48">&#91;48&#93;</a></sup></cite></div></blockquote>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Theology">Theology</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Ignatius_of_Antioch&amp;action=edit&amp;section=13" title="Edit section: Theology">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Christology">Christology</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Ignatius_of_Antioch&amp;action=edit&amp;section=14" title="Edit section: Christology">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3><p>
Ignatius is known to have taught the deity of Christ: <link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r996844942"/></p><blockquote class="templatequote"><p>There is one Physician who is possessed both of flesh and spirit; both made and not made; God existing in flesh; true life in death; both of Mary and of God; first passible and then impassible, even Jesus Christ our Lord.</p><div class="templatequotecite">—&#8201;<cite><i>Letter to the Ephesians</i>, ch. 7, shorter version, Roberts-Donaldson translation</cite></div></blockquote>
<p>The same section in text of the Long Recension says the following:
</p>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r996844942"/><blockquote class="templatequote"><p>But our Physician is the Only true God, the unbegotten and unapproachable, the Lord of all, the Father and Begetter of the only-begotten Son. We have also as a Physician the Lord our God, Jesus the Christ, the only-begotten Son and Word, before time began, but who afterwards became also man, of Mary the virgin. For "the Word was made flesh." Being incorporeal, He was in the body, being impassible, He was in a passible body, being immortal, He was in a mortal body, being life, He became subject to corruption, that He might free our souls from death and corruption, and heal them, and might restore them to health, when they were diseased with ungodliness and wicked lusts.</p><div class="templatequotecite">—&#8201;<cite><i>Letter to the Ephesians</i>, ch. 7, longer version</cite></div></blockquote>
<p>He stressed the value of the <a href="/wiki/Eucharist" title="Eucharist">Eucharist</a>, calling it a "medicine of immortality" (<i>Ignatius to the Ephesians</i> 20:2). The very strong desire for bloody martyrdom in the arena, which Ignatius expresses rather graphically in places, may seem quite odd to the modern reader. An examination of his theology of <a href="/wiki/Soteriology" title="Soteriology">soteriology</a> shows that he regarded salvation as one being free from the powerful fear of death and thus to face martyrdom bravely.<sup id="cite_ref-49" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-49">&#91;49&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Ignatius is claimed to be the first known Christian writer to argue in favor of Christianity's replacement of the <a href="/wiki/Shabbat" title="Shabbat">Sabbath</a> with the <a href="/wiki/Lord%27s_Day" title="Lord&#39;s Day">Lord's Day</a>:
</p>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r996844942"/><blockquote class="templatequote"><p>Be not seduced by strange doctrines nor by antiquated fables, which are profitless. For if even unto this day we live after the manner of Judaism, we avow that we have not received grace. ...If then those who had walked in ancient practices attained unto newness of hope, no longer observing Sabbaths but fashioning their lives after the Lord's day, on which our life also arose through Him ... how shall we be able to live apart from Him?</p><div class="templatequotecite">—&#8201;<cite><i>Ignatius to the Magnesians</i> 8:1, 9:1–2, <a href="/wiki/Joseph_Barber_Lightfoot" class="mw-redirect" title="Joseph Barber Lightfoot">Lightfoot</a> translation.</cite></div></blockquote>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r996844942"/><blockquote class="templatequote"><p>​If, therefore, those who were brought up in the ancient order of things have come to the possession of a new hope, no longer observing the Sabbath, but living in the observance of the Lord's day, on which also our life has sprung up again by Him and by His death—whom some deny, by which mystery we have obtained faith, and therefore endure, that we may be found ​the disciples of Jesus Christ, our only Master—how shall we be able to live apart from Him, whose disciples the prophets themselves in the Spirit did wait for Him as their Teacher? And therefore He whom they rightly waited for, being come, raised them from the dead.</p><div class="templatequotecite">—&#8201;<cite><i><a href="https://en.wikisource.org/wiki/Ante-Nicene_Christian_Library/Epistle_to_the_Magnesians" class="extiw" title="s:Ante-Nicene Christian Library/Epistle to the Magnesians">Letter to the Magnesians</a></i> 9, Roberts and Donaldson translation, p. 189.</cite></div></blockquote>
<p>This passage has provoked textual debate since the only Greek manuscript extant read Κατα κυριακήν ζωήν ζωντες which could be translated "living according to the Lord's life." Most scholars, however, have followed the Latin text (secundum dominicam) omitting ζωήν and translating "living according to Lord's Day".<sup id="cite_ref-50" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-50">&#91;50&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Ecclesiology">Ecclesiology</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Ignatius_of_Antioch&amp;action=edit&amp;section=15" title="Edit section: Ecclesiology">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>Ignatius is the earliest known Christian writer to emphasize <a href="/wiki/Loyalty" title="Loyalty">loyalty</a> to a single <a href="/wiki/Bishop" title="Bishop">bishop</a> in each city (or <a href="/wiki/Diocese" title="Diocese">diocese</a>) who is assisted by both <a href="/wiki/Presbyter" title="Presbyter">presbyters</a> (priests) and <a href="/wiki/Deacon" title="Deacon">deacons</a>. Earlier writings only mention <em>either</em> bishops <em>or</em> presbyters.
</p><p>For instance, his writings on bishops, presbyters and deacons:
</p>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r996844942"/><blockquote class="templatequote"><p>Take care to do all things in harmony with God, with the bishop presiding in the place of God, and with the presbyters in the place of the council of the apostles, and with the deacons, who are most dear to me, entrusted with the business of Jesus Christ, who was with the Father from the beginning and is at last made manifest.</p><div class="templatequotecite">—&#8201;<cite><i>Letter to the Magnesians 2</i>, 6:1</cite></div></blockquote>
<p>He is also responsible for the first known use of the Greek word <i>katholikos</i> (καθολικός), or catholic, meaning "universal", "complete" and "whole" to describe the Church, writing:
</p>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r996844942"/><blockquote class="templatequote"><p>Wherever the bishop appears, there let the people be; as wherever Jesus Christ is, there is the Catholic Church.  It is not lawful to baptize or give communion without the consent of the bishop. On the other hand, whatever has his approval is pleasing to God. Thus, whatever is done will be safe and valid.</p><div class="templatequotecite">—&#8201;<cite><i>Letter to the Smyrnaeans</i> 8, J.R. Willis translation.</cite></div></blockquote>
<p><a href="/wiki/Joseph_Lightfoot" class="mw-redirect" title="Joseph Lightfoot">Joseph Lightfoot</a> states the word "<a href="/wiki/Catholic_(term)" title="Catholic (term)">catholic</a> (καθόλου)" simply means "universal" and can be found not only before and after Ignatius amongst ecclesiastical and classical writers, but centuries before the Christian era.<sup id="cite_ref-51" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-51">&#91;51&#93;</a></sup> Ignatius of Antioch is also attributed the earliest recorded use of the term "<a href="/wiki/Christianity" title="Christianity">Christianity</a>" (Greek: <span lang="el">Χριστιανισμός</span>) <span title="circa">c.</span><span style="white-space:nowrap;">&#8201;100</span>&#160;AD.<sup id="cite_ref-52" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-52">&#91;52&#93;</a></sup> 
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Parallels_with_Peregrinus_Proteus">Parallels with Peregrinus Proteus</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Ignatius_of_Antioch&amp;action=edit&amp;section=16" title="Edit section: Parallels with Peregrinus Proteus">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<p>Several scholars have noted that there are striking similarities between Ignatius and the <a href="/wiki/Christians" title="Christians">Christian</a>-turned-<a href="/wiki/Cynicism_(philosophy)" title="Cynicism (philosophy)">Cynic</a> philosopher <a href="/wiki/Peregrinus_Proteus" title="Peregrinus Proteus">Peregrinus Proteus</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-brent_23-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-brent-23">&#91;23&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-schoedel_53-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-schoedel-53">&#91;53&#93;</a></sup> as described in <a href="/wiki/Lucian" title="Lucian">Lucian</a>'s famous satire <i><a href="/wiki/Passing_of_Peregrinus" title="Passing of Peregrinus">The Passing of Peregrinus</a></i>:
</p>
<ul><li>Both Ignatius and Peregrinus show a morbid eagerness to die.</li>
<li>Both characters are, or have been, Christians.</li>
<li>Both are imprisoned by Roman authorities.</li>
<li>Upon the arrest of both prisoners, Christians from all over Asia Minor come to visit them and bring them gifts (cf. <a rel="nofollow" class="external text" href="http://www.tertullian.org/rpearse/lucian/peregrinus.htm"><i>Peregr.</i></a> 12–13).</li>
<li>Both prisoners sent letters to several Greek cities shortly before their deaths as "testaments, counsels, and laws", appointing "couriers" and "ambassadors" for the purpose.<sup id="cite_ref-schoedel_53-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-schoedel-53">&#91;53&#93;</a></sup></li></ul>
<p>It is generally believed that these parallels are the result of Lucian intentionally copying traits from Ignatius and applying them to his satire of Peregrinus.<sup id="cite_ref-brent_23-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-brent-23">&#91;23&#93;</a></sup><sup class="reference nowrap"><span title="Page / location: 73">&#58;&#8202;73&#8202;</span></sup> If the dependence of Lucian on the Ignatian epistles is accepted, then this places an <a href="/wiki/Terminus_post_quem" title="Terminus post quem">upper limit</a> on the date of the epistles: around the 160s AD, just before <i>The Passing of Peregrinus</i> was written.
</p><p>In 1892, Daniel Völter sought to explain the parallels by proposing that the Ignatian epistles were in fact <i>written</i> by Peregrinus, and later edited to conceal their provenance, but this speculative theory has failed to make a significant impact on the academic community.<sup id="cite_ref-harrison_54-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-harrison-54">&#91;54&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Pseudo-Ignatius">Pseudo-Ignatius</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Ignatius_of_Antioch&amp;action=edit&amp;section=17" title="Edit section: Pseudo-Ignatius">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<p>Epistles attributed to Saint Ignatius but of spurious origin (their author is often called <a href="/wiki/Pseudo-Ignatius" title="Pseudo-Ignatius"><b>Pseudo-Ignatius</b></a> in English) include:<sup id="cite_ref-55" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-55">&#91;55&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<ul><li>Epistle to the Tarsians</li>
<li>Epistle to the Antiochians</li>
<li>Epistle to Hero, a Deacon of Antioch</li>
<li>Epistle to the Philippians</li>
<li>The Epistle of Maria the Proselyte to Ignatius</li>
<li>Epistle to Mary at Neapolis, Zarbus</li>
<li>First Epistle to St. John</li>
<li>Second Epistle to St. John</li>
<li>The Epistle of Ignatius to the Virgin Mary</li></ul>
